impl Counter { fn value(self) -> i64 { return self.n } fn bump(self: mut Counter) -> i64 { self.n = self.n + 1 return self.n } } fn main() -> i64 { var c = Counter { n: 0 } c.bump() return c.value() 10 + c.bump() } trait_dyn — supported trait T { fn m(self) -> T } struct S { .. } impl T for S { fn m(self) -> T { ... } } fn f(x: dyn T) -> T Dynamic dispatch through a 'dyn Trait' parameter. Every declared method must be implemented, and the implementation must match the trait exactly: same parameter count/types and same return type, so a mismatched impl fails with LIR-E3005/LIR-E3004. Receiver forms are 'self', 'self: T', 'self: mut T', '&self', '&mut self'; a trait may declare a mutable receiver only as '&mut self' or 'self: mut T'. Trait methods cannot have default bodies. Values are passed by value and the vtable is selected at the call site. Traits cannot be used as standalone values ('let t: T'), only as 'dyn T' parameters/returns. Bounded integer type. Constant assignments outside the range are compile errors (LIR-E3004); dynamic values are not trapped by this type alone. Monomorphized generics: instantiate explicitly with <T> at call/construction sites. Nested generic arguments such as Option<Option<i64>> do not parse because '>>' lexes as a shift; bind an alias first. Resolves to an embedded std module (core, io, os, sys, net, crypto, json, math, str, algo, testing, collections, hash, fmt, time, http), to a sibling/local .lir file, or to a quoted path. Members are unqualified after import. An unknown name that is not a local file stops compilation with LIR-E4004 and a suggestion. There is no C FFI declaration syntax yet. Use the @syscall/@open/@write family, or compile with --emit-c and link in C. Immutable binding; the annotation is optional and drives type checking. Re-declaring the same name in the same scope is an error (LIR-E3003); use a new scope to shadow. Targets: local variables, struct fields, pointer dereferences and array elements. Assigning to an immutable binding or while it is borrowed is an error. Blocks are always required. Conditions must be bool; '=' inside a condition is reported with a did-you-mean-'==' hint. 'if' is also an expression that yields a value (see if_expression). fn grant(score: i64) -> i64 { if score > 90 { return 3 } elif score > 50 { return 2 } else { return 1 } } Negated 'if' (Elixir-style). It is a value expression as well. Prefer 'if !cond' for audiences unfamiliar with LIR. fn main() -> i64 { let x = 0 unless x == 0 { return 100 / x } else { return 0 } } while — supported Pre-test loop. Provide loop progress: an infinite body that freezes its condition variables is rejected, and break/continue are not allowed inside 'defer'. '..' excludes the end bound, '..=' includes it. Bounds must be integers; both are evaluated once, before the first iteration. The loop variable is immutable. fn main() -> i64 { let mut sum = 0 for i in 0..4 { sum += i } for i in 4..=4 { sum += i } return sum } for_in — supported Iterates a dynamic array (@array_new) or a string byte by byte. The loop variable is the element value. There is no index/value pair form. fn main() -> i64 { let items = @array_new(4, 8) @array_push(items, 5) @array_push(items, 6) let mut sum = 0 for v in items { sum += v } return sum } break_continue — supported fn main() -> i64 { let mut sum = 0 for i in 0..10 { if i == 5 { break } if i % 2 == 0 { continue } sum += i } return sum } return — supported A trailing expression without 'return' is also accepted in value functions; explicit 'return' is the canonical style. Runs on every exit from the enclosing scope, in LIFO order. break/continue/return are rejected inside a deferred block. fn main() -> i64 { defer println("second") defer println("first") return 0 } guard_else — supported Early-exit helper: the else block must leave the function. It does not bind values and does not support 'guard let'. Rejected: raw pointer work is expressed with the T / @read_ / @write_ builtins instead of an unsafe block. Both branches must produce a value of the same type; an 'else' branch is mandatory when a value is used. fn main() -> i64 { let flag = 7 > 3 let v = if flag { 10 } else { 20 } return v } match — supported Expression form: arms are comma separated and produce a value. Matching must be exhaustive: integers need a '_' (or 'else') arm, enums need every variant or a fallback, bools may cover true/false. fn classify(n: i64) -> i64 { return match n { 0 => 100, 1..=9 => 10, v if v > 100 => 1000, _ => 0, } } fn main() -> i64 { return classify(0) + classify(5) + classify(500) + classify(-3) } operators — supported '' is right-associative integer power and binds tighter than unary minus. Arithmetic wraps modulo 2^64 (no overflow trap); division by zero and out-of-range shifts trap. Precedence: '*' > unary > ' / %' > '+ -' > shifts > '&' > '^' > '|' > comparisons > '&&' > '||' > ternary > assignment. fn main() -> i64 { let a = 2 + 3 4 let b = 2 3 2 let c = (5 & 3) | (8 ^ 1) << 1 return a + b + c } boolean_operators — supported fn main() -> i64 { if true and !false { return 1 } if false || true { return 2 } return 0 } ternary — supported Integer casts narrow by keeping the low bits (300 as u8 == 44). Float to integer truncates toward zero and traps on NaN/out-of-range. Integer to float preserves signedness. There is no implicit numeric conversion. fn main() -> i64 { let wide: i64 = 300 let narrow = wide as u8 let back = narrow as i64 let f = 2.75 return back + (f as i64) } string_literal — supported UTF-8, immutable, length-prefixed. Escape sequences: \n \t \r \\ \" \0. Concatenate with '+', compare with '==', index with s[i] (byte value), read s.len. fn main() -> i64 { let s = "lir" + "lang" println(s) if s == "lirlang" { return s.len } return 0 } string_interpolation — supported Any expression inside { } is interpolated. Literal braces must be escaped as \{ and \}. Format specifiers such as {x:#x} are NOT supported (they print literally); use a formatting helper from std::fmt instead. fn main() -> i64 { let a = 2 let b = 3 println("{a}+{b}={a + b}") return 0 } char_literal — supported A char literal is an i64 code point value. There is no distinct 'char' type (annotating with 'char' is rejected). Decimal, hex, binary, octal with '_' separators. Integer literals type-check against their context inside the i64 range; a float literal needs a float context (a bare '1' is not a float). fn main() -> i64 { let a = 0xFF let b = 0b1010 let c = 1_000 return a + b + c } array_literal — supported Fixed-size array on the stack; index with a[i] (runtime bounds-checked, out of range traps). Use @array_new/@array_push for growable arrays. fn main() -> i64 { let a = [10, 20, 30] let zeros = [0; 2] return a[1] + zeros[0] } dynamic_array — supported Heap array of 8-byte slots by default. @array_get is bounds-checked and traps out of range. Free with @array_free (compile-time UAF/double-free analysis covers straight-line code). fn main() -> i64 { let a = @array_new(8, 8) @array_push(a, 4) @array_push(a, 5) let n = @array_len(a) let sum = @array_get(a, 0) + @array_get(a, 1) @array_free(a) return n 10 + sum } tuple — supported Tuples hold up to 8 fields in 8-byte slots; field access is t.0 ... t.7. Destructuring is supported in 'let'. Field types keep their width/signedness. fn main() -> i64 { let (value, ok) = pair() if ok { return value } return 0 } question_mark — supported Propagates None/Err to the enclosing function, whose return type must be Option/Result. Also see '??' for a default value. fn main() -> i64 { let present: ?i64 = Some(3) let missing: ?i64 = None return (present ?? 7) 10 + (missing ?? 7) } closure — supported Pipe-delimited lambdas with capture by value of surrounding locals. '||' introduces a no-argument closure. A closure body may be an expression or a block. fn main() -> i64 { let step = 2 let add = |x: i64| x + step let seven = || 7 return add(seven()) } method_call — supported Methods come from 'impl'. Field access and method calls share syntax, so a struct field that has the same name as a method is ambiguous (methods win). Named/out-of-order arguments are allowed on direct free-function calls only. Evaluation follows source order. Defaults may reference global constants. Builds a dynamic array from a range (with optional '..='), an array or a string, optionally filtered with 'if'. The result is a heap array; free it with @array_free. fn main() -> i64 { let squares = [i i for i in 0..4] let evens = [i for i in 0..10 if i % 2 == 0] let a = @array_len(squares) let b = @array_len(evens) @array_free(squares) @array_free(evens) return a 10 + b } map_literal — supported Literal map construction; '#' followed by '{' is the disambiguator. The same as @map_new + @map_set. fn main() -> i64 { let m = #{ "a": 3 } let v = @map_get(m, "a") @map_free(m) return v } spawn — supported Starts an OS thread (host targets only: x86_64/aarch64/riscv64 Linux, macOS, Windows, FreeBSD). The thread body may take one i64 argument passed by value; spawn { ... } and closures take none. Passing pointers to locals into a thread is rejected. Join with @thread_join, which yields the body's return value. wasm32-wasi has no OS threads: the compiler rejects thread/channel builtins there with LIR-E7004. Bounded MPMC channel of 8-byte values. @chan_recv blocks until a value is available. Requires a host target with OS threads: wasm32-wasi rejects it with LIR-E7004. fn main() -> i64 { let c = @chan_new(1) @chan_send(c, 6) let v = @chan_recv(c) @chan_close(c) return v } atomic — supported Sequentially consistent operations on 8-byte aligned memory. @atomic_add returns the previous value. fn main() -> i64 { let slot = @malloc(8) @atomic_add(slot, 2) let v = @atomic_add(slot, 3) @free(slot) return v } comptime — supported
<veriayrimi>
Compile-time evaluation of pure constant expressions and comptime functions. Loops and recursion are supported with fixed bounds; I/O and runtime values are not. Some(v)/None are context-typed: 'None' needs a typed local, return type or '?' context (a bare 'let x = None' is rejected with 'add type annotation'). Read the payload with match or '?'. Note '?i64' is also accepted as a type spelling. Ok(v)/Err(e) with '?' propagation to the caller. '??' supplies a default for Option. The error payload is 8 bytes (i64 or pointer). Typed pointers to locals, structs and heap memory. '&x' borrows read-only, '&mut x' borrows mutably and blocks reads/writes of x while live. Returning a pointer to a local is a compile error. Raw addresses use @ptr_add/@read_/@write_. The '[]T' spelling parses and type-checks, and a dynamic array can be passed to it, but there is no slice-of-slice slicing operator and no '.len' on slices (use @array_len on the array). fn main() -> i64 { let a = @array_new(4, 8) @array_push(a, 9) return first(a) } fn_pointer — supported Function values are first class: pass named functions, store them in locals, and call them through the binding. Struct fields cannot hold them (the field is then read as a method). Hash map with i64 or string keys depending on the first key used; keys are hashed with a per-process secret (HashDoS safe). @map_get returns 0 for a missing key: check @map_has first. fn main() -> i64 { let m = @map_new() @map_set(m, 1, 40) @map_set(m, 2, 2) let total = @map_get(m, 1) + @map_get(m, 2) @map_free(m) return total } integer_types — supported All are 8-byte slots internally; annotations drive signedness/width checks and casts. i128/u128/i54-style widths do not exist. fn main() -> i64 { let a: u8 = 200 let b: i32 = -5 let c: usize = 3 return (a as i64) + (b as i64) + (c as i64) } float_types — partial Arithmetic, comparisons and casting work; float literal values are stored as binary64 and 'f32' annotations accept float literals only through arithmetic contexts (a bare integer literal is not a float). Floats print with printFloat/printlnFloat. A function without '-> T' returns void. 'never' marks functions that do not return normally (exit/panic). A bool is a distinct type; it does not implicitly convert to i64. Use (a and b), comparisons, or @signum for arithmetic keys. fn main() -> i64 { let flag: bool = 3 < 4 if flag { return 1 } return 0 } Attributes attr_test — supported [test] fn t() { @assert_eq(1, 1) } Marks a test function collected by 'lir test'. Test functions take no parameters and do not need main. [test] fn adds() { @assert_eq(2 + 2, 4) } attr_inline — supported [inline] Inline hint for the optimizer. #[cold] is the inverse hint. [inline] fn fast(x: i64) -> i64 { return x 2 } Declares a function free of side effects. Calling a printing/allocating builtin inside a pure function is rejected, which lets the optimizer CSE identical calls. Hoare-style contract checked at compile time when arguments are constant (a statically violated call fails with 'precondition violation'), otherwise as a runtime assertion. Avoid: requires/ensures keywords (use #[pre]/#[post]) [pre(b != 0)] fn divide(a: i64, b: i64) -> i64 { return a / b } Status: design contract / implementation backlog. This document does not claim that features are already implemented. A feature becomes supported only when parsing, semantic validation, code generation, diagnostics, tests, and documentation agree. Product contract LIR is intended to be a compiled, low-level systems language implemented by the Zig-based LIR compiler. It must emit native object/executable code directly through LIR backends, not silently delegate compilation to GCC, Clang, LLVM, or another compiler. The first supported target remains Linux x86-64 ELF until additional targets have independent conformance tests. The language must provide predictable layout and execution, explicit control over resource use, useful safety checks, and an opt-in route to unsafe machine-level operations. It must not promise that every program is memory-safe or that generated code can never contain defects. Core type system Required primitive types: Signed and unsigned fixed-width integers: i8/i16/i32/i64/i128 and u8/u16/u32/u64/u128, plus target-sized isize/usize. IEEE floating-point f32/f64; bool; void; never-confusable char/byte types; and explicitly specified string/slice types. Arrays with compile-time length, slices with runtime length, tuples where useful, enums with explicit or inferred integer tags, structs, unions, and opaque types. Optional values and result/error values must have specified layout and control-flow semantics; no implicit nullable raw pointers. Explicit casts and checked conversions. Truncating, sign-changing, pointer/integer, and reinterpretation casts must be explicit operations. Acceptance criteria: type-check every expression before code generation; reject mismatched types, invalid casts, unresolved names, duplicate declarations, invalid return paths, and unsupported operations with source-spanned diagnostics. No unsupported construct may be silently skipped. Pointer and memory model Required pointer forms and operations: Single-object pointers, raw/many-item pointers, optional pointers, immutable and mutable references, and slices with explicit length. Pointer arithmetic only in documented unsafe contexts unless proven bounds-safe by the compiler. Explicit address-of, dereference, pointer casts, integer-address conversion, alignment queries, and alignment assumptions. A clear distinction between uninitialized storage, initialized values, zeroed storage, and invalid/unreachable states. Explicit allocation APIs and allocator parameters; no hidden garbage collector or mandatory runtime. Stack allocation, fixed-size arrays, caller-provided buffers, arenas, pools, and custom allocators can be expressed without hidden heap allocation. defer/scope cleanup must have deterministic LIFO semantics, including early returns and error exits. volatile access for device memory and externally observable memory; volatile is not an atomicity guarantee. Atomic types and operations with a documented memory-order model. Unsupported target operations must fail at compile time or use a documented legal lowering. Safety profile: Safe code checks bounds, alignment, initialization, integer overflow where required by the chosen mode, and lifetime/aliasing rules that the compiler actually implements. unsafe marks the boundary for raw pointer arithmetic, arbitrary address access, inline assembly, and other operations whose preconditions cannot be proven. Provide explicit build modes: Debug/Safe (checks enabled), ReleaseSafe (checks retained), and ReleaseFast (only checks whose removal is documented). Optimization must never change defined program semantics. Deterministic data layout and ABI Required features: Default struct layout with documented alignment and padding rules. extern struct/union for a specified foreign ABI; packed struct for exact bit layout; explicit field alignment and explicit enum tag type. Compile-time size, alignment, field-offset, and bit-size queries. Layout assertions that fail compilation when an ABI contract is violated. Explicit function calling conventions: LIR-native and C ABI at minimum for the initial target. Exported symbols, external declarations, symbol visibility, name mangling rules, variadic foreign calls only when correctly supported, and a stable rule for aggregate arguments/returns. A documented stack alignment rule, callee/caller-saved register contract, red-zone policy, unwind/debug metadata policy, and return-value convention per target. No ABI feature is considered supported until tests cover calls both from LIR to foreign code and from foreign code into exported LIR functions. Hardware-level facilities Required low-level facilities, introduced incrementally and gated by target support: Inline assembly with explicit input/output operands, clobber sets, volatility, and target constraints; reject malformed or unsupported constraints. CPU feature detection and target-feature selection, with runtime dispatch only when the program explicitly requests it. SIMD/vector types and intrinsics with defined lane types, alignment, and fallback policy. Atomic read/write, compare-exchange, read-modify-write, fences, and memory orderings. Bit operations, rotates, overflow-reporting arithmetic, endian conversion, and explicit unaligned loads/stores. MMIO-compatible volatile loads/stores and target-specific barriers. Never imply that a volatile access is atomic. OS system-call interfaces as target-specific libraries/examples, not portable language semantics. Freestanding entry points, no-default-runtime builds, explicit linker scripts, custom sections, symbol retention, and static/no-stdlib linking where the target supports them. Interrupt/exception entry attributes only for targets whose calling convention and code-generation rules are fully implemented. Compile-time and abstraction features Compile-time constants and evaluation with bounded execution, diagnostics, and deterministic semantics. Generic functions/types with compile-time parameters and monomorphization as the initial implementation strategy. Type aliases, modules, imports, visibility, and explicit external interfaces. Traits/interfaces and implementations only after method resolution and coherence/conflict rules are specified. No mandatory object runtime, dynamic dispatch, RTTI, exception runtime, or hidden allocation. Any dynamic dispatch must be explicit and have a documented representation. Intrinsics are compiler-recognized, typed, target-gated operations—not ordinary identifiers that can silently become unresolved calls. Integer, arithmetic, and optimization semantics
<veriayrimi>
Every integer operation must specify overflow behavior. The implementation must distinguish wrapping, checked, saturating (if provided), and undefined/invalid operations. Division by zero, signed minimum divided by -1, invalid shift widths, and out-of-range conversions must have defined diagnostic/runtime behavior for each build mode. Optimizer rules: Only transform verified IR. Preserve signedness, overflow semantics, aliasing, volatile/atomic effects, call effects, and control-flow behavior. Do not remove or reorder observable operations. Run IR verification after lowering and after each optimization pass in compiler-debug/test builds. Add regression tests for every optimizer bug and differential tests against a simple reference evaluator where possible. Native compiler pipeline and backend contract Required pipeline: Source manager and lexer with byte offsets, line/column mapping, and malformed-input recovery. Parser that either produces a complete valid syntax tree or diagnostics; no silent skipping of declarations. Name resolution and module/import graph validation. Complete type checking and target-independent semantic validation. Typed, canonical IR with explicit effects and control-flow terminators. IR verifier: valid IDs, types, block targets, terminators, dominance/use-def rules where applicable, and valid call signatures. Semantics-preserving optimization passes. Machine IR, liveness analysis, register allocation with spilling, calling-convention lowering, and stack-frame layout. Instruction encoding with range/relocation validation. Object/ELF writer with checked arithmetic, segment/section validation, deterministic output, and atomic file replacement. Runtime execution tests for generated binaries; malformed or unsupported programs must not produce a successful output artifact. The current bootstrap target is Linux x86-64 ELF. Do not label other targets supported until each has a complete backend and end-to-end tests. Tooling required for production use Stable CLI: build, run, check, test, fmt (when formatter exists), version, target listing, and diagnostic/JSON diagnostic modes. Exit codes and stderr/stdout behavior documented and tested. Reproducible builds, clear versioning, source locations in diagnostics, and no panics for user input. Unit, parser recovery, semantic-negative, IR-verifier, optimizer, ABI, ELF validation, integration, fuzz, and regression tests. CI for debug and optimized configurations; test generated executables, not only compiler exit codes. Benchmarks that compare equivalent results, record compiler versions/flags/CPU, use repeated runs, and measure compile time, runtime, binary size, and peak memory. Documentation for the language grammar, ABI, memory model, safety modes, target support, standard library, examples, and feature status. Release gates A feature is not production-ready until all relevant gates pass: Grammar and semantic rules documented. Positive and negative tests added. Malformed input cannot crash the compiler or write a misleading output. IR invariants and target constraints are checked. Generated code is executed in integration tests. ABI and layout are checked against a reference where applicable. Debug and optimized builds pass CI. Feature status in README/spec is accurate. Implementation order Make the current subset honest and fail-closed: stop silently skipping enum, trait, impl, mod, const, type, extern, or unsupported syntax. Establish source spans, structured diagnostics, parser recovery, name resolution, and a real semantic/type-checking phase. Verify canonical IR and make code generation reject any invalid or unsupported IR operation. Complete integer semantics, local variables, functions, control flow, arrays/slices, and pointer rules end to end. Implement deterministic layout and the initial C ABI, with executable interop tests. Implement safe/unsafe boundaries, allocator APIs, defer semantics, and no-runtime/freestanding mode. Add atomics, volatile/MMIO, SIMD, inline assembly, and target intrinsics one capability at a time. Add generics and compile-time evaluation after type checking and resource limits are reliable. Complete register allocation/spilling, relocations, debug/unwind metadata, and reproducible performance benchmarks. Add another target only after the target-independent semantic contract and backend conformance suite are stable. The priority is a small, fully implemented and testable low-level core—not a large keyword list whose constructs are ignored by the compiler. Proposed low-level syntax and keyword surface These words are reserved by the lexer but are not yet supported language features. Until each feature has parser, semantic-analysis, IR, backend, diagnostics, and end-to-end tests, the compiler must reject its use rather than silently accept it or emit incorrect machine code. The syntax sketches are not a promise of current support. Before implementation, settle precedence, grammar, layout, aliasing, volatile semantics, and target-dependent type widths in the specification. Implemented bitwise and shift operators The bootstrap compiler now parses and lowers integer bitwise AND (&), OR (|), XOR (^), complement (~), left shift (<<), and arithmetic right shift (>>) through AST, IR, and the Linux x86-64 encoder. Shift operators have lower precedence than addition and higher precedence than relational comparisons; bitwise operators have separate precedence levels below equality and above logical conjunction/disjunction. The current backend uses signed 64-bit integer values, so >> is arithmetic right shift. These operators do not imply checked shift-count semantics yet; target x86-64 masks the hardware shift count. The full typed integer model and portable overflow/shift diagnostics remain future work. Implemented unreachable trap The unreachable statement is now parsed as a statement, lowered to a dedicated IR operation, and encoded as x86-64 ud2. Reaching it intentionally traps rather than silently returning a value. It is a runtime trap in this bootstrap implementation, not yet a proof-backed optimizer assumption or a never type. Terminator correctness The IR generator stops lowering a statement list after a terminator and does not append an implicit return after the unreachable statement. This prevents the trap from being treated as an ordinary non-terminal instruction. A regression test covers an unreachable statement followed by a return. A feature-by-feature comparison between LIR (this repository, bootstrap compiler lir 2.3.0) and Odin (Bill Hall, 2016). How to read the evidence column LIR claims were produced by running this branch's compiler — lir check / lir run --backend=vm — on each construct. Every "supported" line corresponds to an entry in lir lang --json that zig build test compiles. Odin claims come from the official overview, the language FAQ and the community style guide (odin-styleguide). No Odin toolchain was available while writing this, so Odin snippets are illustrative, not executed here. Where LIR is weaker, it says so. docs/STATUS.md is the authoritative gap list. Both languages are C replacements for people who want the machine to stay visible: no GC, no exceptions, no hidden control flow, compile to native code. Odin is the mature, data-oriented one: implicit context allocators, #soa layout, built-in vector/matrix/quaternion maths, distinct types, or_return error propagation, an excellent C FFI, and a decade of production use (EmberGen, Blick and others). LIR is the small, self-describing one: a deliberately tiny core (65 supported constructs, 71 reserved words, 131 builtins) whose compiler publishes its own machine-readable catalogue, emits coded diagnostics with machine-fixable expect{kind,value} metadata, and ships a portable IR VM so a generated program can be validated and run with no toolchain at all. Put differently: Odin optimises for the programmer's throughput and cache behaviour; LIR optimises for a generator's ability to be correct on the first try. impl Counter { fn get(self) -> i64 { return self.value } fn bump(self: mut Counter) -> i64 { self.value = self.value + 1 return self.value } } // Odin — illustrative (free procedures, no methods, p->x auto-deref) Counter :: struct { value: i64, } counter_get :: proc(c: Counter) -> i64 { return c.value } counter_bump :: proc(c: ^Counter) -> i64 { c.value += 1 return c.value } main :: proc() { c := Counter{} // zero-initialised counter_bump(&c) fmt.println("value=", counter_get(c)) } What differs in kind, not detail: LIR groups behaviour under the type (impl); Odin deliberately does not. Odin's Counter{} is a valid zero value; LIR requires every field (Counter { } is LIR-E3004 missing field 'x'). Odin's fmt.println is variadic and formats anything; LIR's println takes a string and printInt/printFloat take numbers — the type checker rejects println(42). 3.2 Error handling // LIR — ? propagation, ?? default, exhaustive match on Result fn parse(s: string) -> Result<i64, string> { if @string_len(s) == 0 { return Err("empty") } return Ok(@string_to_int(s)) } fn divide(a: i64, b: i64) -> Result<i64, string> { if b == 0 { return Err("division by zero") } return Ok(a / b) } fn main() -> i64 { let v = match divide(20, 4) { Ok(n) => n, Err(_) => -1, } let d = parse("") ?? 7 // default on Err printInt(v + d) println("") return 0 } parse :: proc(s: string) -> (value: i64, err: Parse_Error) { if len(s) == 0 { return 0, .Empty } return strconv.parse_i64(s) or_return } divide :: proc(a, b: i64) -> (n: i64, err: Parse_Error) { if b == 0 { return 0, .Not_A_Number } return a / b, nil } main :: proc() { n, err := divide(20, 4) if err != nil { n = -1 } // or_return in a proc with matching named results: // v := parse("42") or_return }
<veriayrimi>
Both refuse exceptions. LIR models failure as a value in the type system (Result<T,E>, so a match must handle both cases); Odin keeps it a convention enforced by exhaustiveness on the error enum and the or_return shorthand. 3.3 Generics fn main() -> i64 { let b = Box<i64> { value: 3 } printInt(max_of<i64>(b.value, 7)) println("") return 0 } Odin infers the type parameter from the argument; LIR requires the explicit <i64> at call/construction sites (its monomorphiser is deliberately literal). Odin's typeid/[]$E constraints have no LIR equivalent. 3.4 Cleanup fn main() -> i64 { { defer println("inner block done") println("body") } defer println("function done") return 0 } // verified output: body / inner block done / function done Odin's defer also runs at end of scope, so this is a genuine match rather than a LIR invention. LIR additionally statically rejects the classic mistakes — double free, use-after-free, defer conflict, a break inside defer, and (verified) a heap allocation assigned inside a loop that leaks on the next iteration. Where Odin is clearly ahead Memory ergonomics. Odin's implicit context carries the allocator and a temp arena into every procedure, so make/new work in library code you don't control and arenas are a one-liner. LIR has raw @malloc/@free and no allocator abstraction at all. Zero-init by default. Odin's "zero is initialization" removes a whole bug class. LIR forces explicit initialisation but leaves heap memory uninitialised. Data-oriented toolkit. #soa, array programming, swizzles, built-in vector/matrix/quaternion types, bit_set. LIR has none of these; a SIMD/SoA workload is simply not LIR's territory yet. distinct types. type Celsius = distinct i64 buys unit safety for free. In LIR an alias is transparent and let c: Celsius = 5 is unguarded. Slices. x[:], x[1..3], for &x in xs. LIR accepts []T but rejects range slicing (LIR-E2001 expected rbracket, got dot_dot) — this is the single most annoying difference when porting Odin-shaped code. C FFI. foreign import + #link_name is a first-class feature and the reason Odin ships DirectX/Metal/Vulkan/OpenGL bindings. LIR rejects extern outright (fail-closed) and can only reach the OS through builtins. Ecosystem and maturity. Ten years, a large core:/vendor: library, odinfmt, ols, hot reload, real games shipped. LIR is a bootstrap compiler with 131 builtins, 16 embedded modules, no formatter and no LSP. Auto-dereference ergonomics. p.x on a ^T just works in Odin; LIR needs (p).x-style explicitness in places and self: mut Self for mutating methods. Where LIR is clearly ahead Machine-readable language definition. lir lang --json returns every construct with a status, notes, an avoid list and a compiled example; lir lang --keywords returns the reserved-word list from the lexer; lir lang --cheatsheet renders the whole language to one page. Odin publishes prose plus a community style guide. For an LLM writing code, the LIR catalogue is directly consumable; Odin's is not. Diagnostics that are repairable by a program. Every LIR error has a stable code, a span, a help: line, and usually expect{kind,value}: error[LIR-E3002]: call to undefined function 'printint'; did you mean 'printInt'? = help: declare the function with 'fn', or import the module that defines it expect = {"kind":"function","value":"printInt"} --diagnostics=short and --json (lir.diagnostics.v1) exist as first-class contracts. Odin's compiler errors are human prose. Compile-time ownership analysis. LIR rejects, before code generation: use-after-free, double free, defer/close conflicts, leaks from allocations inside loops, dangling returns of pointers to locals, capturing a local by reference into a thread, mutation of captured variables inside spawn, and unsynchronised writes to shared pointers inside spawn (@atomic_/mutex required). Odin has none of this; it relies on the programmer plus -vet. Contracts, purity and bounded ranges in the language. [pre(expr)], #[post(result …)], #[pure], type Percent = i64 range 0..100 — verified for constant arguments at compile time. Odin offers assert/ ensure at runtime only. Concurrency in the language, statically gated. spawn, channels, mutexes and atomics are keywords/builtins, and a construct the target cannot provide is a compile error (LIR-E7004 for threads on wasm32-wasi) rather than a runtime trap. Odin's equivalent lives in core:thread/core:sync. A portable VM backend. lir run f.lir --backend=vm executes on any host with identical semantics and traps — no C toolchain, no linker, no binary. That is what makes "generate → validate → execute" a tight loop for an agent. Cross-target reach without a toolchain. x86-64 ELF and wasm32-wasi are emitted directly; 8 further triples go through C11 when zig/cc is present. Odin targets desktop OSes natively and is still finishing WASM. Fail-closed philosophy. Anything unimplemented is rejected with a code and the catalogue marks it unsupported with an avoid note — no silent miscompilation, and no documentation that drifts (CI diffs the generated reference and cheatsheet against the compiler). 5b. "OOP" in either language Neither language is object-oriented, but they are non-OOP in different ways, which is the more interesting question than a yes/no. impl Counter { fn get(self) -> i64 { return self.value } // method fn bump(self: mut Counter) -> i64 { // mutating method self.value = self.value + 1 return self.value } } trait Area { fn area(self) -> i64 } // interface struct Tile { side: i64 } impl Area for Tile { // implementation fn area(self) -> i64 { return self.side self.side } } fn measure(s: dyn Area) -> i64 { return s.area() } // dynamic dispatch fn main() -> i64 { var c = Counter { value: 0 } c.bump() // 1 let tile = Tile { side: 3 } return c.get() + measure(tile) // 1 + 9 = 10 } So: Odin rejects OOP entirely and gives you composition (using) instead; LIR accepts the method/interface half of OOP and rejects the inheritance half. Practically, Odin code is procedural by construction, while LIR code can look familiar to someone coming from C#/Java — with the important difference that dyn Trait dispatch is a parameter type, not a heap-allocated object: measure(s) passes the struct by value and the vtable is chosen at the call site. Two practical notes verified on this build: a &mut self method requires the binding to be mutable (var c = ...; calling it on an immutable let is LIR-E3009), and Counter { value: 0 } must name every field. For AI generation this matters: an LIR model can use familiar shapes (struct + impl + trait), but must never emit extends, : Base, using, default trait bodies, let x: Trait, or operators on structs — all of which are compile errors with stable codes rather than silent misbehaviour. Philosophy in one line each What LIR should borrow (and what it should not) Worth borrowing, in rough order of value: Slices — x[:], x[a:b], for &x in xs. Currently the biggest gap for porting and for idiomatic array code. (slice_type is already marked partial in the catalogue.) Allocator abstraction — even a single Allocator struct passed explicitly would let libraries be allocator-agnostic without Odin's implicit context. distinct types — cheap to implement (a flag on the alias), high payoff for unit-safety, and easy to describe in the catalogue. Zero-init for heap allocations — one builtin (@calloc) or a documented default; removes a class of generated-code bugs. Multiple return values — already supported (-> (i64, i64) is verified); worth promoting in the catalogue and docs as the idiomatic error channel. Not worth copying: no-closures (LIR's closures are used by spawn and comprehensions), no-methods (LIR's impl keeps dyn Trait dispatch simple), and the implicit context (it conflicts with LIR's "explicit and checkable" stance — though docs/STATUS.md notes an allocator interface as a possible future). Choosing between them Use Odin for games, engines, graphics, simulation, anything needing C libraries, SoA layout, hot reload, or a decade-proven toolchain. Use LIR when the code is generated or reviewed by machines, when you want a program validated and executed with no toolchain (--backend=vm), when you want the compiler to refuse unsafe patterns instead of trusting you, and when a stable machine contract (lir.diagnostics.v1, LIR-Exxxx) matters more than a large standard library. Porting Odin → LIR: replace methods with free functions or keep impl (LIR supports it), replace distinct with plain aliases (and lose the checking), replace slices with []T plus explicit indices, replace context allocators with explicit @malloc/@free, and replace or_return with ?. LIR side verified with lir 2.3.0 on this branch: zig build test 115/115, tests/ai_test.py green, examples/canonical-tour.lir byte-identical on vm/native/c/wasm. Odin side reflects the official documentation and the community style guide; nothing there was executed in this environment. This is project organization and CLI tooling, not a claim that every application framework or runtime is implemented. Start and run The starter contains project.manifest, src/main.lir, src/lib.lir, src/tests.lir, and .gitignore. Main and tests import the same library. Creation refuses existing destinations rather than overwriting files. build, run, check, ir, and test accept an explicit source file, an explicit project directory, or no input (current directory's manifest). An explicit file bypasses the manifest, preserving single-file use. Runtime arguments still follow --; backend, target, and optimization flags remain CLI options. Import paths resolve relative to the importing source file, including nested project entries. Existing hello-world and web-server manifests remain readable; that does not imply their application domains are fully supported. Schema
<veriayrimi>
{ "name": "demo", "version": "0.1.0", "source_dir": "src", "entry": "main.lir", "test_entry": "tests.lir" } name is required and uses the same safe-name validation as lir init. source_dir defaults to src, relative to the project directory. entry defaults to main.lir, relative to source_dir. test_entry is relative to source_dir. If omitted, test uses an existing source_dir/tests.lir. If neither exists, it fails with MissingTestEntry; it does not silently run the application's entry. Explicit source-file testing is unchanged. version is optional descriptive text; it does not select dependencies. Legacy description (string), targets, domains, imports, permissions (string arrays) are accepted metadata only. They do not configure targets, resolve packages, grant permissions, or sandbox execution. Unknown keys, duplicate fields, wrong JSON types, malformed JSON and manifests over 64 KiB fail. Source paths must be nonempty relative paths, without .. components, backslashes, colons, NUL, or empty slash-separated components. source_dir: "." is allowed. These are lexical checks, not a symlink or import security boundary. Only build trusted projects. Project builds default to <project>/<name>.out plus the target's .exe or .wasm suffix; --emit-c defaults to <project>/<name>.c. Explicit -o wins and remains relative to the caller's working directory. Explicit source-file builds retain their previous a.out/a.c defaults. No build scripts or implicit package downloads execute. Validation python3 tests/project_test.py checks the generated project across native, VM, C, and Wasm backends; current-directory and directory inputs; nested imports, runtime arguments, output overrides and target suffixes; invalid manifests, missing tests, and explicit-file compatibility. C needs a host compiler; Wasm execution needs Node or Wasmtime, just as the backend suite does. Next foundations for broader application types The initial browser Wasm bridge now demonstrates event-driven execution with JavaScript-owned DOM and events; it is not a full frontend stack. This increment supplies a common project entry point. It does not add HTTP, GUI, mobile packaging, database drivers, shared-library linking, or .NET/CLR. Further independent work is needed: Versioned package/dependency resolution, lockfiles and offline/reproducible builds, with integrity validation. Stable standard-library I/O, filesystem, networking and error contracts, with backend capability checks and tests. Explicit library artifacts and safe FFI/linking contracts before native framework integrations. Web/service, desktop and mobile integrations with platform-specific build, signing, deployment and CI validation. Runtime/language facilities (memory ownership or GC strategy, structured errors, async/concurrency) as specified and tested features, not metadata. This review examined the bootstrap build, CLI, project scaffolding, compiler driver, IR verifier, test infrastructure, and published implementation claims. It is not a complete security audit of the roughly 40,000-line bootstrap or a certification of backend parity. Features in the roadmap still require end-to-end evidence. Implemented improvements zig build run -- <args> now forwards arguments to the compiler. lir init demo creates demo/project.manifest and demo/src/main.lir, matching the Quick Start instructions, instead of writing into the current directory. Initialization refuses existing directories (including empty ones), files, unsafe names, and . / ... It uses exclusive file creation and removes its newly created project directory if creation fails. Existing projects are never intentionally overwritten. This is not a sandbox against a malicious process concurrently modifying the parent directory. check, ir, and test reject extra positional arguments; ir rejects unknown options; init rejects extra arguments. run deliberately retains program-argument forwarding; use -- to explicitly separate program arguments. Added isolated starter-project unit tests and a Python standard-library CLI regression suite, wired into CI. Tests exercise metadata, argument errors, generated-project checking, VM execution, native/C emission, and preservation of an existing artifact on compilation failure. Run the checks zig build zig build test python3 tests/cli_test.py ./zig-out/bin/lir python3 tests/backend_test.py ./zig-out/bin/lir zig build test -Doptimize=ReleaseSafe zig build test -Doptimize=ReleaseFast zig build run -- version The unit suite executes Linux ELF artifacts and is currently Linux-oriented. The CLI regression suite uses a temporary directory and a 30-second subprocess limit; compiler paths are resolved before changing the working directory. Next priorities (not implemented by this change) Backend semantic parity: expand differential native/VM/C/Wasm tests for signed division, overflow, shifts, arrays, strings, traps, and call ABI. A file being emitted successfully does not establish equivalent execution semantics. Optimization coverage: CLI build, run, and test now honor -O0 instead of silently promoting it to -O2. Continue extending the optimization matrix to advanced features and error paths before making performance claims. IR verification: structural verification is present, but needs a systematic audit of register definitions/dominance, phi predecessor correspondence, terminators, call signatures, and consistency with backend frame limits. Safety claims: full ownership/lifetime checking is explicitly unfinished. Audit each README safety claim with positive and negative runtime tests for each backend before treating it as a language-wide guarantee. Portable testing: separate Linux ELF execution tests from portable frontend tests; add macOS/Windows host build and execution jobs before promising parity. Project system: connect manifest consumption, dependency resolution, and test discovery to the bootstrap CLI. Scaffolding a manifest is not evidence of a complete package manager. Version consistency: CLI reports 2.2.0 while build.zig.zon reports 2.0.0; select a release version policy and derive both from a single source. Avoid adding more syntax before these correctness gates and reproducible performance baselines are established. Ninth development pass: virtual-register bounds verification The structural IR verifier now rejects every nonzero destination, operand, or call/phi argument register whose index is outside that function's declared virtual-register table. This check runs before and after optimization and prevents malformed IR from reaching register allocation/code generation with an invalid index. It does not yet verify definition-before-use, dominance, or complete phi predecessor correspondence; those remain explicit audit items. A negative unit test covers an out-of-range destination, and the ReleaseFast compiler plus the backend/core/CLI/call/project/browser suites pass after the change. Second development pass: optimization and execution parity Removed silent -O0 promotion from build, run, and test. The default remains -O2; semantic checks and mandatory lowering still run at -O0. Added tests/backend_test.py: four independent expected-output fixtures run on native ELF, IR VM, C11, and Wasm at -O0, -O1, and -O2 (48 executions). Coverage includes recursion, nonzero return status, signed division/remainder, shifts, branches, loop-carried values, strings, and indexed arrays. Added a separate IR comparison regression that distinguishes -O0 from -O2 and checks build --emit-ir -O0 against ir -O0. Output equivalence alone would not detect silently substituted optimization levels. The Wasm matrix exposed a Node runner bug: wasi.start() returned the program's exit status, but the JS runner discarded it. The runner now assigns that result to process.exitCode, so nonzero status reaches the calling shell. This was reproduced at all three optimization levels and the matrix passes after the fix. CI installs Node 22 and runs the backend matrix. A C toolchain and Node 22+ or Wasmtime are required locally. These are selected correctness fixtures, not a proof of complete backend parity or a performance benchmark. Third development pass: language features Implemented loop statements, numeric unary plus, right-associative i64 **, and eight integer intrinsics. See Language extensions for exact contracts and intentional restrictions. Runtime power uses bounded exponentiation by squaring with a negative-exponent trap; constant evaluation and AST folding share the wrapping-power helper. All new operations lower to existing IR, so no backend-specific opcode implementations are required. The backend matrix now has nine positive fixtures (108 executions), 12 negative runtime exponent checks, and compile-time diagnostics for invalid uses. A native float-print interior-pointer free discovered during testing was also corrected. Fourth development pass: selected core-language phase Added nested tuple patterns, typed arm-local bindings, and guarded wildcards. Strengthened match result/guard/pattern checks, enum payload arity validation, range typing and unsigned comparisons, tuple destructuring arity, inferred tuple metadata, and recursive tuple compatibility. Unsupported alternative bindings and non-binding enum payload patterns now fail explicitly rather than being silently miscompiled. See Core language phase 1 for compatibility changes, tests, and remaining generic-inference work. Validation for this phase: 91/91 Debug unit tests; 24 core fixture executions and 25 negative frontend cases; existing backend and CLI suites. The core execution matrix also passes using Zig's C compiler. This is not a full borrow-checker or complete type-inference implementation. Fifth development pass: recursive enum payload patterns
<veriayrimi>
Replaced binding-only enum payload analysis with recursive, field-typed pattern analysis. Literal, tuple, nested constructor, and constant integer range payloads are supported. IR lowering gates payload field loads on a successful constructor tag comparison and recursively binds names only in a fully matched arm. Constructor exhaustiveness now depends on irrefutable payloads: a pattern such as Some(7) cannot cover the entire Some variant. The coverage model deliberately remains conservative rather than attempting a full pattern-space solver. The core suite now runs three fixtures (36 backend/optimization executions) and 31 negative frontend cases. The new fixture also exercises range endpoints, failed nested guards, string payloads, and payload-free unmatched variants. Validation: 91/91 Debug unit tests, core/backend/CLI Python suites, and the core matrix with Zig's C compiler all passed. See CORE_LANGUAGE.md for the current contract; earlier pass descriptions above are historical snapshots. Sixth development pass: C#-inspired call ergonomics Added named arguments and trailing constant-default parameters for direct free functions. Semantic resolution records argument-to-parameter mappings without reordering source evaluation; IR lowering evaluates supplied arguments once in source order, then arranges values and fills omitted defaults. Imported defaults are resolved in declaration context. Simple generic inference also resolves named arguments to declaration parameters before inferring their types. Unsupported named call categories and nonconstant/ill-typed defaults fail explicitly. Extended calls are rejected during scalar compile-time evaluation rather than being folded before semantic checks. This is deliberately not a CLR, .NET library bridge, managed object model, or claim of full C# compatibility. Validation: 91/91 Debug unit tests; 24 call-suite backend/optimization executions; 20 invalid call/default programs; existing core/backend/CLI suites. The new call matrix also passes using Zig's C compiler. See CSHARP_SCOPE.md for contracts, limitations, and the missing runtime/ecosystem capabilities. Seventh pass: manifest-driven project foundation Added typed, size-limited manifest loading and consistent optional file/directory selection for build/run/check/ir/test. Project output names derive from the validated manifest name; explicit file and output paths retain their contracts. The starter now shares a library between main and an independent test entry. Unknown/invalid settings fail closed; legacy metadata does not execute scripts, resolve dependencies or enforce permissions. Path validation is lexical, not a sandbox. Missing project tests produce an error instead of running main. The project integration suite covers all four execution backends, custom nested entries/imports, argument forwarding, generated artifacts, manifest failures and single-file compatibility. See PROJECTS.md for scope and remaining milestones. Eighth pass: browser execution bridge Added an opt-in JavaScript host for the emitter's seven existing WASI imports, without changing the compiler's WASI target or CLI execution semantics. Validates memory ranges, preserves streaming Unicode, uses only Web Crypto randomness, and distinguishes intentional exits from traps. The counter example executes actual LIR arithmetic on each DOM event; DOM and events remain explicit JS glue. The host is synchronous, single-use and not a resource sandbox. Node-based conformance tests and real O0/O1/O2 compiler-output tests supplement existing backend/project regressions; no browser UI automation is claimed. The implementation sequence and mandatory correctness/performance gates are specified in Compiler Architecture. The complete low-level language contract (including pointer/memory semantics, ABI/layout, hardware facilities, safety modes, tooling, and release gates) is in Low-Level Language Specification. Roadmap checkboxes are not evidence of correctness by themselves: mark an item complete only after its tests run successfully in CI and the behavior is integrated end-to-end. Release blockers include unchecked capacity/offset arithmetic, process termination from reusable compiler stages, silent acceptance of unsupported syntax, invalid IR reaching code generation, incorrect ABI/register/stack behavior, and non-atomic executable output. Validated status (2026-11): zig build test passes 115/115 unit + catalogue-conformance tests, and tests/{cli,core,call,project,ai,backend,browser}_test.py all pass against the release build. Recent bootstrap hardening: unsupported top-level declarations and non-i64 parameter/return/struct-field annotations now fail closed; AST source-span access uses overflow-safe range validation. These changes reduce silent miscompilation risk but do not constitute a complete type checker. The IR string table now checks both entry and byte capacities before writes, deduplicates only validated ranges, and causes compilation to fail explicitly on overflow. A structural IR verifier now runs before native code generation and checks function/source ranges, block and instruction counts, branch targets, string references, call argument bounds, struct layout, parameter/local slot bounds, and backend stack-frame limits. Regression tests cover malformed branch/string references, invalid local slots, and oversized stack frames. The earlier test-module name-shadowing compile error is fixed and covered by the current green run. [x] Defer statement codegen [x] Compile-time evaluation (comptime, @size_of, @align_of, @type_name) Phase 3: Advanced Optimizer [x] SSA-based CFG IR with structural verifier (ir_verify.zig) [x] Global value numbering (ir_opt.zig) [x] Loop invariant code motion (ir_opt.zig) [x] Inline expansion (ir_opt.zig) [x] Tail call optimization [ ] Escape analysis [ ] Vectorization (SIMD) [ ] Profile-guided optimization Phase 3 notes: soundness fixes and the -O1 pipeline (2026-11) Working on runtime performance surfaced three soundness bugs. All three were present before this branch, all three produced a wrong answer while every level reported success, and all three are fixed here. The fixes are the only reason the -O1 numbers below can be trusted at all. unrollCountedLoops advanced the induction variable once per unrolled repetition rather than once per iteration. It also emitted one write-back copy per body definition instead of one per variable, so a later copy overwrote an earlier one. while n <= 10 { count += 1; n += 1 } printed 6 instead of 9 at -O2. Fixed by chaining repetitions through the vreg map and writing back only the last definition of each carried variable. coalesceCopiesPostSSA pass B rewrote a result into a destination dst that was still read further down the block, and pass A renamed a definition into a destination that already had one. Together they turned x = x + 1; y = x + 1; x = y + 1 into x = x + 1; y = x + 1; x = x + 1. Fixed by requiring a single definition of dst and no reads of dst between the two instructions. rotateLoops ran on functions that still carried phis and emitted ill-formed code (SIGILL at runtime). It now refuses when any phi is present. tests/core_test.py::LoopOptimizationSoundnessTests covers each case at all three levels, on the native and VM backends. With those closed, -O1 was rebuilt: it previously ran local passes only, which left parameters and loop variables in memory and made the same function up to 2.4x slower than at -O2. It now builds SSA, runs if-conversion, copy propagation and coalescing, rotates and unrolls counted loops, and skips only inlining, global value numbering and loop-invariant hoisting. Code generation also follows trivial copy chains when looking for a constant divisor, so x / 10000 keeps its multiply-based lowering after out-of-SSA (mandelbrot had regressed 12% purely from a v = copy const in front of a div). Paired measurement, 21 sample pairs per workload, bootstrap 95% CI, old and new binaries alternating within each pair (old = this branch before the change, same machine, same session): Geometric mean of the eight ratios: 1.13x. -O1 compile time rises from 2.5 ms to 3.7 ms net on the 255-function module, still 2x faster than -O2 at 7.6 ms. Only the "yes" rows are claimed; the rest are noise this machine cannot resolve. Phase 3 notes: six more wrong-answer bugs found by heavier workloads (2026-11) Writing the LIR-vs-Rust math suite (bench/rust_compare, eleven programs) was worth more as a bug hunt than as a benchmark: six further miscompiles surfaced, none of which the existing corpus reached. All shared one property - they only appear when a program combines constructs the small tests keep apart - and all produced a plausible-looking number rather than a crash.
<veriayrimi>
Two were in code generation, and both were register-allocation assumptions: The constant-divisor % / / fast path parks the magic multiplier in rcx and remembers it in cur_rcx_const, which codegen used to hoist the load out of loops. But rcx is the implicit divisor register for div: a runtime div in the same loop (mov %rdi,%rcx; div %ecx) overwrote the parked magic, so the next iteration multiplied by the runtime divisor. ((i13)%100) plus 100000000/d2 in one loop printed -7284561654821 instead of -10000000000. Hoisting removed; each division loads its magic inline. Measured cost: none (geomean 0.99x over bench/speed_compare). The same path used r10 as scratch when the dividend was spilled. r10 is in CALLER_SAVED_POOL and can hold a live vreg - in a nested loop it held the outer induction variable, which the inner % 100 destroyed. It now uses r11, which really is reserved (rax/rcx/rdx/rsp/rbp/r11). Four more were in the optimizer, all about phis: mem2reg placed phis for every slot before inserting any of them, and renaming consults a per-slot promotable[] flag. A slot rolled back for block-capacity reasons could still have its names in the shared pool, so an operand-less phi reached the CFG and the vreg it defined was never assigned. Placement now commits per slot (pending -> placed). simplifyTrivialPhis rewrote a phi as dest = copy v unconditionally. A phi is defined on every incoming edge, a copy only where it executes. The rewrite now requires definedBefore (real dominators). globalCopyPropagate rewrote phi operands. An operand names the value live on one specific edge; replacing {16, 18} with {16, 9} because some block contained copy 18, 9 makes the merge read a stale register. This is what turned a = b; b = t - m | b = t into an exploding recurrence at -O2 on the native and VM backends (06_fib_mod printed 735302655874374793 instead of 577847172). Phi operands are now left alone. The -O0 build disagreed with -O1/-O2 on literal modulo (1234 % 100 printed -666) because of (2). tests/core_test.py::LoopOptimizationSoundnessTests now has seven cases; the three added here and the phi pair added with the benchmark suite all fail on the parent commit and pass after these fixes. 16 benchmark programs (10 in speed_compare, 6 at the time of the fix in rust_compare) are identical across -O0/-O1/-O2. Phase 3 notes: compile-time work (2026-11) Compilation is the axis where the gains in this branch are unambiguous and reproducible, because they are pure work-removal (no code generation change: -O0 and -O2 output is byte-identical to the previous compiler on the test corpus). Measured with tools/compile_bench.py, which subtracts process startup and alternates the two binaries sample by sample, on a 255-function module (the compiler's per-unit function cap) of four-statement helpers: In-process phase timers on the same input: IR optimization 2067 -> ~1200 us and machine-code + ELF emission 2672 -> ~620 us. The remaining per-unit cost is front-end dominated (lexing/parsing ~470 us, sema ~300 us, IR lowering ~1000 us, codegen ~600 us), i.e. ordinary single-pass work rather than redundant table traffic. The changes are all of one kind: several passes are written against fixed-capacity tables (4096 vregs, 192x384 instruction slots) and cleared them in full on every invocation, many times per function. See src/bootstrap/ir_opt.zig (deadCodeElimination generation stamp, localConstantFoldAndLVN dirty list + block-generation stamp, globalCopyPropagateSmall) and src/bootstrap/ir_codegen.zig (allocRegsForFn/analyzeElidedConsts bounded by IRFn.vreg_count). Runtime quality is unchanged by this work. Wall-clock ratios on this shared VM move by more than the effects being measured (absolute throughput drifted ~1.8x between two runs of identical binaries), so the runtime table in the README is left as measured earlier rather than restated from a noisy sample. Phase 3 notes: measured gaps (2026-11) Recorded so optimizer work is tracked against evidence rather than assumed. Numbers come from bench/speed_compare measured with tools/ab_bench.py (interleaved paired sampling, median of 13, 3 warmups, ratio = LIR/Zig, 95% bootstrap CI); every program is also checked to print byte-identical output to its Zig reference. Inner-loop multiplies move the accumulator first (mov %A,%B then imul, plus mov %B,%C / sar to keep the product) where a memory-form imul mem,reg or commuting imul rax,%A would avoid a copy. This is the visible difference on the multiply-heavy kernels (mandelbrot 1.11x, bitwise_hash 1.14x, branch_mix 1.16x). Note that a naive "extra mov" count is misleading: 1 + 21 folds to 3 at -O2 and Zig independently emits the same mov-pair shape for h = h ^ (h >> 32), so the gap is in operand selection, not in the optimizer. Multi-block loops are not unrolled. unrollCountedLoops only matches a single-block self-loop of at most 14 instructions, so a counted loop whose body spans two blocks (variable initialisation plus an if/break) is left alone. Zig unrolls exactly those loops twice (mandelbrot's inner loop appears as two 26-instruction versions against one 38-instruction version), so the unroll is the measurable structural difference on that kernel. bitwise_hash_20m keeps its loop bound in a register because the bound is a function parameter, where Zig receives the same value as an immediate through inlining; that is an inlining-context difference rather than a compare-selection defect. Compiler throughput is not the bottleneck: compile time is 1400-2100x faster than zig build-exe -OReleaseFast on the same programs (median of 15, warm cache for Zig), so the runtime gap above is a code-quality question. extension [ ] IntelliJ plugin [ ] Documentation generator [x] REPL (lir repl interactive Read-Eval-Print Loop) [ ] Playground (web-based) Phase 7: Advanced Features (2027+) [ ] Comptime reflection [ ] DSL support (macros) [ ] Effect system [ ] Algebraic effects [ ] Dependent types (optional) [ ] Proof assistant integration [ ] Formal verification Performance and Usability Goals These are engineering targets, not verified claims about the current compiler. Record baseline measurements before changing the optimizer, and keep each optimization only when correctness tests pass and benchmarks show a repeatable improvement. Required optimization workflow Establish a reproducible baseline against a recent, optimizing C compiler (for example, Clang or GCC) using documented flags and identical workloads. Include microbenchmarks and representative programs; prevent dead-code elimination from invalidating results and verify outputs for every implementation. Run multiple repetitions, report median and spread, and test both debug and release configurations. Inspect generated assembly for claimed low-level wins; measure compile time, binary size, and memory alongside runtime. Never claim “faster than C” universally. State exactly which workload, hardware, compiler versions, flags, and correctness constraints support each result. Language Design Principles Simplicity first — Prefer a small set of consistent rules over many special cases. Easy to read and write — Infer obvious local types; require annotations where inference would be ambiguous or obscure. Performance by construction — Predictable layouts, explicit allocation control, and abstractions that optimize away when possible. Safety without hidden costs — Enforce ownership and borrowing in the type checker; do not claim safety until checks are implemented and tested. Optimize from evidence — Use benchmarks, generated-code inspection, and regression tests rather than intuition alone. Portability through backends — Keep language semantics separate from target-specific code generation. Honest status — Clearly distinguish implemented, partial, and planned features.
<veriayrimi>
Version: 2.3 (bootstrap reference) · compiler lir 2.3.0 Status of this document: describes what the checked-in bootstrap compiler actually accepts, checks and executes. Anything not implemented is explicitly labelled planned (see ROADMAP.md). Ground truth. The authoritative, always-current list of syntax is generated from the compiler itself: sh lir lang # human-readable catalogue with examples lir lang --json # machine-readable (schema lir.lang.v1) lir lang --keywords # reserved words (schema lir.keywords.v1 with --json) lir lang --markdown # regenerate docs/LANGUAGE_REFERENCE.md lir builtins --json # every builtin + signature lir check file.lir --json # validate; lir.diagnostics.v1 on stderr LANGUAGE_REFERENCE.md is generated from that table and every supported example is compiled and executed by zig build test, so it cannot silently drift from the implementation. LIR is a small, performance-first systems language. Its reference implementation, Ziglir, is a Zig bootstrap compiler that emits: Design goals (not guarantees): a small and consistent core syntax, predictable layout and allocation, checks that are on by default, and evidence-backed performance claims. 1.1 One-page example fn area(shape: Shape) -> i64 { return match shape { Shape::Square(side) => side side, Shape::Empty => 0, } } fn main() -> i64 { let mut total = 0 for i in 1..=3 { total += area(Shape::Square(i)) } println("total = {total}") return 0 } Lexical structure Encoding: UTF-8 source; identifiers are ASCII [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]. Statement separation: newlines. ; is accepted and ignored, but is never required and must not be placed between if/else blocks. Comments: # to end of line, // to end of line, /// documentation comment, and nested / ... /. #[ introduces an attribute and #{ a map literal, so they are not comments. Literals: integers — decimal, 0xFF, 0b1010, 0o17, with _ separators; must fit in i64 (diagnostic LIR-E1004 otherwise) floats — 1.5, 2.5e3 (binary64 value; f32 rounding only on cast) strings — "..." with \n \t \r \\ \" \0; interpolation "{expr}" chars — 'A' is an i64 code point, not a distinct type booleans — true, false; null exists only for pointer contexts i8 i16 i32 i64 u8 u16 u32 u64 isize usize integers (8-byte slot) f32 f64 IEEE-754 bool distinct from integers string immutable, length-prefixed UTF-8 void never no value / does not return (T, U, ...) tuple, up to 8 fields, accessed as t.0 … t.7 [T; N] [v; N] fixed-size array / repeated element []T slice spelling (parses and type-checks; no slicing syntax) T mut T pointer to a value; &x / &mut x borrow a local fn(A) -> R function value Option<T> ?T optional value Result<T, E> success or error (8-byte payloads) Name / Name::V struct/enum/union type and variant constructor dyn Trait dynamic dispatch map chan builtin handles (@map_, @chan_) 3.3 Conversions let wide: i64 = 300 let byte = wide as u8 // 44 — low 8 bits are kept let back = byte as i64 // 44 let n = 2.9 as i64 // 2 — truncation toward zero float → integer traps on NaN and out-of-range values; integer → float preserves signedness (unsigned values stay non-negative); f32 casts round to binary32 precision before widening; a bare integer literal is not a float: let f: f64 = 1 is rejected. fn add(a: i64, b: i64) -> i64 { return a + b } const LIMIT: i64 = 3 * 4 struct Point { x: i64 y: i64 } // newline-separated fields enum Shape { Square(i64), Empty } // comma-separated variants union Bits { i: i64 f: f64 } trait Area { fn area(self) -> i64 } impl Area for Square { fn area(self) -> i64 { return self.side self.side } } impl Point { fn norm1(self) -> i64 { return self.x + self.y } } type Count = i64 type Percent = i64 range 0..100 import math import "../lib/util.lir" Rules: functions are top-level only; there are no nested function declarations (use a closure |x| ... for local behaviour); at most 8 parameters per function; defaults must follow the parameter and may reference global constants; struct fields are newline separated and cannot name another struct type — store B instead. Struct literals use commas: Point { x: 1, y: 2 }; proc is a legacy alias of fn; pub is accepted and ignored (there is no visibility system); mod, extern, and extern "C" are rejected (LIR-E2003). let x = 1 // immutable let x: i64 = 1 // annotated let mut n = 0 // mutable (legacy: var n = 0) const K = 2 5 // local compile-time constant n += 1 // += -= = /= %= = &= |= ^= <<= >>= if c { } elif c2 { } else { } unless c { } else { } // negated if; value-producing while c { } // pre-test loop, requires progress until c { } // loop until the condition holds loop { break } // unconditional loop for i in 0..n { } // exclusive range for i in 0..=n { } // inclusive range for v in array { } // element iteration (arrays and strings) break continue // loops only, not inside defer return value defer cleanup() // LIFO, runs on every scope exit guard cond else { return } unreachable match value { … } // expression, see §6 There are no labelled loops, no break value, no do/while, no for(;;), and no index/value pair form (for i, v in arr is rejected). Precedence (high → low): → unary + - ! ~ & → / % → + - → << >> → & → ^ → | → comparisons → and → or → ?: → ?? → assignment. match is an expression; arms are comma separated and must be exhaustive (integers need _ or else, enums need every variant or a fallback, guarded arms never establish coverage). Memory, ownership and safety Local variables live on the stack; @malloc/@array_new/@map_new allocate heap memory and must be released (@free, @array_free, @map_free). Borrowing: &x and &mut x. While a mutable borrow is live, reading or writing the owner is a compile error; returning a pointer to a local is rejected. Compile-time analyses that run by default: use-after-free, double-free, double-close, leaks inside loops, constant index out of bounds, constant invalid shift counts, division by a constant zero, dangling references, stack-frame budget (32 KiB), spin loops that cannot terminate. Runtime traps: bounds violations, division/modulo by zero, dynamic shift overflow, NaN/out-of-range float→int conversions, heap canary corruption. A trap terminates the process with status 132. Raw memory is reached only through @ptr_add, @read_u8, @write_u64, …; there is no unsafe { } block and no inline assembly. A full Rust-style borrow checker and lifetime inference are not implemented; ROADMAP.md tracks that work. fn twice(ok: bool) -> Option<i64> { let v = find(ok)? // propagate None to the caller return Some(v * 2) } fn main() -> i64 { let a: ?i64 = None let fallback = a ?? 7 // 7 return match twice(true) { Some(v) => v, None => 0 } } Some/None/Ok/Err are context typed — let x = None without an annotation or return context is rejected with a hint to add a type. Attributes [test] // collected by lir test [inline] #[cold] // optimization hints [pure] // side-effect-free; printing/allocating is rejected [pre(b != 0)] // precondition, verified when arguments are constant [post(result >= 0)] // postcondition lir build file.lir -o app [-O0|-O1|-O2] [--target=…] [--backend=…] [--emit-c] lir run file.lir [--backend=vm|native|wasm|c] [-- args…] lir check file.lir [--json] lir test [file.lir] lir ir file.lir [-O2] # dump SSA IR lir ast file.lir # pretty-print the syntax tree lir lang [feature] [--json|--markdown] # language catalogue lir builtins [name] [--json] # builtin catalogue lir explain <topic|LIR-Exxxx> # knowledge base lir init name [--ai] # new project (+ agent scaffolding) lir targets | repl | install | version | help Diagnostics carry a stable code, a span, and usually a help line plus an expect{kind, value} field for automated repair: file.lir:2:5: error[LIR-E3004]: annotation on 'x' is 'i64' but the initializer has type 'string' 2 | let x: i64 = "text" Language surface counts, generated: 65 supported, 2 partial, 3 legacy, 6 explicitly-unsupported entries (lir lang --short); 71 reserved words (lir lang --keywords). Per-area detail and the known-gap list live in STATUS.md. Single, honest matrix of what the bootstrap compiler does today. lir lang --json is authoritative; this page is the human summary and is kept in sync by tests/ai_test.py (which fails if a supported example stops compiling, or an unsupported probe starts compiling). Every backend runs examples/canonical-tour.lir with byte-identical stdout (asserted in tests/ai_test.py). Diagnostics/traps are supposed to match too; see the known gaps below. Language Known gaps (tracked, not hidden) Full borrow/lifetime checking — only the conservative subset above. Option<Option<T>> and a[1][0]-style nesting need a local binding first. Contract verification is constant-only; runtime checks are not emitted. No DWARF, no package manager, no LSP. Threads/channels are unavailable on wasm32-wasi by design (LIR-E7004). Cross-targets through the C11 backend need zig (or a matching CC) on PATH; without it the compiler fails with CrossCompilerNotFound and a hint instead of emitting a host binary. The IR structural verifier (ir_verify.zig) checks shapes, indices and branch targets, but not def-before-use ordering or dominance. Six wrong-answer bugs in codegen and in the phi handling of mem2reg / simplifyTrivialPhis / globalCopyPropagate were found by hand in 2026-11 (see ROADMAP Phase 3); a real dominance-checking verifier would have caught four of them automatically. It is the next soundness item, ahead of any further optimization work. LIR ships a zero-wrapper standard library: the modules are plain LIR source embedded in the compiler executable (src/bootstrap/stdlib.zig) and inlined at compile time. There is nothing to install, no runtime to link, and no version skew between compiler and library.
<veriayrimi>
fn main() -> i64 { println(str_trim(" lir ")) // "lir" — modules are not namespaced let ring = rb_new(8) // ring buffer from collections rb_push(ring, 1) printInt(gcd_i64(12, 18)) // 6 — from algo println("") println(hex_encode("hi")) // 6869 — from fmt rb_free(ring) // explicit, no GC return 0 } Rules that follow from that design: import <name> must be the first statement of the file, one module per line. Members are not namespaced: import str gives you str_trim, not str.trim. Name collisions with your own functions are reported by sema. Every function is ordinary LIR — you can read the source with lir ast/--emit-ir, and step it in --backend=vm. Allocating helpers return raw pointers and require explicit @free/ @_free. Nothing is garbage collected. A module must be imported to be available; importing an unknown name fails at compile time with LIR-E4001 and a suggestion. The compiler rejects an unknown module at compile time rather than silently emitting a stub — see lir explain errors. Functions that can fail return a sentinel (-1, 0, null pointer) rather than raising; check the return value. Option/Result are the idiomatic wrapper when you want ?/?? handling. Writing new library code Library code follows the same rules as user code: no extern, no unsafe, no asm. It is compiled by zig build test through the catalogue conformance tests, so a module that stops compiling breaks CI. Add new modules to src/bootstrap/stdlib.zig and langref.zig's import note, then regenerate docs/LANGUAGE_REFERENCE.md.

// Lir Self-Hosting Compiler
// Written in Lir itself — compiled by the bootstrap compiler
// This is the official Lir compiler: lir-compile
//
// Supports: structs, enums, traits, impls, generics, comptime,
//           match, async/await, defer, lambdas, error handling,
//           ownership, pattern matching, modules, and more.

import core

// ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
// Token Types
// ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

enum TokenKind {
    // Literals
    IntLit
    FloatLit
    StringLit
    CharLit
    TrueLit
    FalseLit
    NullLit
    Ident

    // Keywords — Structural
    KwFn           // fn
    KwLet          // let
    KwMut          // mut
    KwConst        // const
    KwStruct       // struct
    KwEnum         // enum
    KwTrait        // trait
    KwImpl         // impl
    KwMatch        // match
    KwIf           // if
    KwElse         // else
    KwWhile        // while
    KwFor          // for
    KwIn           // in
    KwReturn       // return
    KwBreak        // break
    KwContinue     // continue
    KwYield        // yield
    KwDefer        // defer
    KwGo           // go (goroutine-style concurrency)
    KwAsync        // async
    KwAwait        // await
    KwTry          // try
    KwRecover      // recover
    KwPanic        // panic
    KwType         // type (type alias)
    KwWhere        // where (generic constraints)

    // Keywords — Modules
    KwPub          // pub
    KwMod          // mod
    KwUse          // use
    KwImport       // import
    KwPull         // pull
    KwAs           // as

    // Keywords — Memory
    KwExtern       // extern
    KwUnsafe       // unsafe
    KwSafe         // safe
    KwStatic       // static
    KwBox          // box (heap allocation)
    KwRc           // rc (reference counting)
    KwArc          // arc (atomic reference counting)
    KwOwned        // owned
    KwBorrowed     // borrowed
    KwClone        // clone
    KwDrop         // drop
    KwSelf         // self

    // Keywords — Compile-time
    KwComptime     // comptime
    KwComptimeIf   // comptimeif

    // Operators — Arithmetic
    Plus           // +
    Minus          // -
    Star           // *
    Slash          // /
    Percent        // %
    StarStar       // ** (power)
    PlusPlus       // ++ (concat)

    // Operators — Comparison
    Eq             // ==
    Ne             // !=
    Lt             // <
    Gt             // >
    Le             // <=
    Ge             // >=

    // Operators — Assignment
    Assign         // =
    PlusAssign     // +=
    MinusAssign    // -=
    StarAssign     // *=
    SlashAssign    // /=
    PercentAssign  // %=
    AmpAssign      // &=
    PipeAssign     // |=

    // Operators — Logical
    AndAnd         // && (and)
    PipePipe       // || (or)
    Bang           // ! (not)

    // Operators — Bitwise
    Amp            // & (bitwise and)
    Pipe           // | (bitwise or or lambda separator)
    Tilde          // ~ (bitwise not)
    Caret          // ^ (xor)
